Statistical-Computational Tradeoffs in Mixed Sparse Linear Regression
Gabriel Arpino, Ramji Venkataramanan

Abstract
We consider the problem of mixed sparse linear regression with two components, where two k k -sparse signals 𝜷 1 , 𝜷 2 ∈ ℝ p {\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p} are to be recovered from n n unlabelled noisy linear measurements. The sparsity is allowed to be sublinear in the dimension ( k = o ⁡ ( p ) k=o(p) ), and the additive noise is assumed to be independent Gaussian with variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} . Prior work has shown that the problem suffers from a k SNR 2 \frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}} -to- k 2 SNR 2 \frac{k^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}} statistical-to-computational gap, resembling other computationally challenging high-dimensional inference problems such as Sparse PCA and Robust Sparse Mean Estimation ( Brennan and Bresler 2020b ) ; here SNR := ‖ 𝜷 1 ‖ 2 / σ 2 = ‖ 𝜷 2 ‖ 2 / σ 2 \texttt{SNR}:=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\|_{2}/{\sigma^{2}}=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\|_{2}/{\sigma^{2}} is the signal-to-noise ratio. We establish the existence of a more extensive k SNR 2 \frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}} -to- k 2 ​ ( SNR + 1 ) 2 SNR 2 \frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}} computational barrier for this problem through the method of low-degree polynomials, but show that the problem is computationally hard only in a very narrow symmetric parameter regime. We identify a smooth information-computation tradeoff between the sample complexity n n and runtime exp ⁡ ( Θ ~ ​ ( k 2 ​ ( SNR + 1 ) 2 / ( n ​ SNR 2 ) ) CLOSE \exp(\tilde{\Theta}(k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}/(n\texttt{SNR}^{2})) for any randomized algorithm in this hard regime. Via a simple reduction, this provides novel rigorous evidence for the existence of a computational barrier to solving exact support recovery in sparse phase retrieval with sample complexity n = o ~ ​ ( k 2 ) n=\tilde{o}(k^{2}) . Our second contribution is to analyze a simple thresholding algorithm which, outside of the narrow regime where the problem is hard, solves the associated mixed regression detection problem in O ⁡ ( n ​ p ) O(np) time and matches the sample complexity required for (non-mixed) sparse linear regression of k ⁡ ( SNR + 1 ) SNR ​ log ⁡ p \frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{p} ; this allows the recovery problem to be subsequently solved by state-of-the-art techniques from the dense case. As a special case of our results, we show that this simple algorithm is order-optimal among a large family of algorithms in solving exact signed support recovery in sparse linear regression. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first thorough study of the interplay between mixture symmetry, signal sparsity, and their joint impact on the computational hardness of mixed sparse linear regression.



1
Introduction

This work considers the problem of two-component mixed sparse linear regression (
\mathtt{MSLR}
), where the goal is to estimate two
k
-sparse signals
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
from
n

unlabelled
noisy linear measurements. The model is defined as follows.

Definition 1.1

(
\mathtt{MSLR}
)
.

For
{\bm{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
,
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
{\bm{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the model:

{\bm{y}}={\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}},

where
\odot
denotes element-wise product between vectors,
X_{i,j}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
w_{i}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
,
z_{i}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\text{Bernoulli}(\phi)
, and
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
each
k
-sparse. Given
({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
the objective is to estimate
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
.

This model was introduced by
Quandt and Ramsey 1978
and has since been widely studied in the machine learning and statistics communities; see, e.g.,
Städler et al. 2010
;
Chen et al. 2014
;
Yi et al. 2014
;
Fan et al. 2018
;
Javanmard et al. 2022
and the references therein.

If the latent variables
(z_{i})_{i\in[n]}
are observed, the problem reduces to solving two separate linear regressions. However, in many applications, the latent variables may be unknown as the data may come from different unlabelled sub-populations. The
\mathtt{MSLR}
model captures this effect and has been applied to a variety of settings including market segmentation
(
Wedel and Kamakura 2000
)
, music perception
(
Viele and Tong 2002
)
, health care
(
Deb and Holmes 2000
;
Luo et al. 2022
;
Im et al. 2022
)
, and various others
(
Li et al. 2022
;
Kazor and Hering 2019
)
.
Variants of mixed regression models called hierarchical mixtures-of-experts have long been studied in the machine learning community
(
Jordan and Jacobs 1994
)
, where they have been used for ensemble learning, and in Gated Recurrent Units and Attention Networks
(
Makkuva et al. 2019
)
.

The maximum-likelihood estimator is a natural choice for estimating the signals
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
. However, the resulting optimization problem is non-convex and NP-hard
(
Yi et al. 2014
)
. The problem is therefore challenging both statistically and computationally, and a variety of efficient estimators have been proposed. These include spectral methods
(
Chaganty and Liang 2013
;
Yi et al. 2014
;
Zhang et al. 2022
)
, expectation-maximization (EM)
(
Khalili and Chen 2007
;
Faria and Soromenho 2010
;
Städler et al. 2010
)
, alternating minimization
(
Yi et al. 2014
;
Shen and Sanghavi 2019
;
Ghosh and Kannan 2020
)
, convex relaxation
(
Chen et al. 2014
)
, moment descent methods
(
Li and Liang 2018
;
Chen et al. 2020
)
, and the use of tractable non-convex objectives
(
Zhong et al. 2016
;
Barik and Honorio 2022
)
.

Despite recent works addressing the statistical and computational feasibility of mixed linear regression (including but not limited to
Azizyan et al. 2013
;
Pal et al. 2022
;
Pal et al. 2021
), little is understood about the problem in the high-dimensional sparse regime where both the sample size
n
and the sparsity
k
can be sublinear in the dimension
p
, over the range of all
SNR
scalings, where
\texttt{SNR}:=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\|^{2}_{2}/\sigma^{2}=\|{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\|^{2}_{2}/\sigma^{2}
is the signal-to-noise ratio. This regime is motivated by a variety of recent statistical applications, ranging from biology to communications (we refer to the monographs
Hastie et al. 2015
;
Giraud 2021
which contain multiple references). The assumptions in Definition
1.1
of i.i.d. Gaussian data rows
{\bm{x}}_{i}
and additive Gaussian noise
w_{i}
have been often considered broadly in the high-dimensional statistics literature as an idealized assumption (e.g.,
Wainwright 2009a
;
Wainwright 2009b
;
Arias-Castro et al. 2011
;
Janson et al. 2017
).

One aspect of this formulation that is starting to become clear is that in a symmetric parameter regime, the
\mathtt{MSLR}
problem is hard, i.e., it cannot be solved by polynomial-time algorithms at the information-theoretically optimal sample complexity
n_{\texttt{IT}}=\tilde{\Theta}(k/\texttt{SNR}^{2})

(
Fan et al. 2018
)
. Exhaustive search typically yields statistically near-optimal estimators for the signal support sets, but the running time is exponential in
k
.
The recent works of
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Fan et al. 2018
provided different ways of quantifying this phenomenon, evidencing a fundamental algorithmic barrier for algorithms performing at all sample complexities
n=\tilde{o}(k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2})
and sparsities
k=o(\sqrt{p})
in a very narrow and symmetric parameter regime which we call
Symmetric Balanced Mixture of Sparse Linear Regressions
(
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
), defined as

\displaystyle\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}:\;\phi=1/2\text{ and }{\bm{\beta}}_{1}=-{\bm{\beta}}_{2}.

(1)

(Here we recall that
\phi
is the mixture parameter in Definition
1.1
, so
\phi=\frac{1}{2}
implies that each
y_{i}
is equally likely to come from
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
or
{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
.)
This phenomenon has been termed a
\frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
-to-
\frac{k^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}

statistical-to-computational gap
, where the problem is solvable with order
k/\texttt{SNR}^{2}
samples, but efficient algorithms require at least order
k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2}
samples. (Throughout this paper, by efficient algorithms we mean those with running time

O(p^{\eta})
for some constant
\eta>0
.) This computational threshold is similar in order to those derived for a multitude of statistical estimation problems, from variants of Planted Clique, e.g., sparse PCA and robust mean estimation
(
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
)
.
Notably,
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
is close to a prominent formulation of sparse phase retrieval where
{\bm{y}}=|{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}|+{\bm{w}}

(
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Fan et al. 2018
)
, which has been widely studied and is believed to possess a
k
-to-
k^{2}
statistical-computational gap
(
Liu et al. 2021
;
Wu and Rebeschini 2021
)
.

The special case of sparse linear regression (
\mathtt{SLR}
), where there is only one signal (i.e.,
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}={\bm{\beta}}_{2}
in Definition
1.1
)
has been extensively studied in the last few decades
(
Candes and Tao 2005
;
Donoho 2006
;
Wainwright 2009b
)
. For
\mathtt{SLR}
, the statistical-computational gap is much smaller, but still exists. Indeed, in the regime where
k=o(p)
, the information-theoretically optimal sample complexity for
\mathtt{SLR}
is of order
\frac{k\log(p/k)}{\log(1+\texttt{SNR})}

(
Wang et al. 2010
;
Reeves et al. 2019
)
; in contrast, recent works such as
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
have established lower bounds in the regime
\texttt{SNR}\to\infty
via the study of the Overlap Gap Property and Low Degree polynomials, and shown that a sample complexity of order at least
k\log{p}
is required for efficiently solving
\mathtt{SLR}
. Moreover, upper bounds of the same order can be obtained using a number of algorithms
(
Wainwright 2009b
;
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
)
.

In this paper, for both
\mathtt{MSLR}
and the special case of
\mathtt{SLR}
, we present new algorithmic lower bounds as well as upper bounds obtained by analyzing a simple thresholding algorithm. The thresholding algorithm, which we call
\mathtt{CORR}
, was used by
Bandeira et al. 2022
to obtain upper bounds for approximate support recovery (up to
o(k)
errors) in
\mathtt{SLR}
, in the setting of binary signal and
\texttt{SNR}\to\infty
with growing
k
. In all our results, we make the dependence on
SNR
explicit, so that they hold for all
SNR
regimes, including
\texttt{SNR}=\Theta(1)
and for
\texttt{SNR}=o(1)
. Before summarizing our results, we define the class of prior distributions we consider for the signals
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
.

Signal Priors

We consider joint priors for
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
that are marginally uniform over
k
-sparse vectors
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
with equal norm
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}
. The case where the two signals have equal norm is more challenging as each entry of the observation
{\bm{y}}
will have the same variance regardless of which signal it corresponds to. We denote such a prior by
\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
, where the non-zero entries of each vector take values in
\mathcal{D}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. We assume that
{\bm{\beta}}_{\texttt{min}}:=\min\{|\beta|\;|\;\beta\in\mathcal{D}\}>0
.

Notation

We use boldface font for vectors and matrices and plain font to denote scalars (e.g.
\bm{a}
and
a
, respectively). For
{\bm{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times p}
,
{\bm{x}}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th row of this matrix and
{\bm{X}}_{j}
the
j
-th column of this matrix. Throughout the work, we adopt the standard asymptotic notation
O(\cdot),\Omega(\cdot),o(\cdot),\omega(\cdot),\text{ and }\Theta(\cdot)
. We let
\tilde{O}(\cdot)
and analogous variants denote these relations up to
polylog
factors. By
\lesssim
,
\gtrsim
,
\simeq
we denote inequalities and equality up to constants, respectively. We let
[n]:=\{1,2\cdots n\}
. For the
\mathtt{MSLR}
setting in Definition
1.1
and the parameter regime
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
in (
1
), we let
\mathtt{MSLR}\setminus\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}
refer to the
\mathtt{MSLR}
problem with associated parameters lying outside the
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
parameter regime.

1.1
Our Contributions

In what follows, our computational lower bound results hold in full generality for signals with bounded amplitude in the scaling regime
p\to\infty
,
n\to\infty
and
k=o(\sqrt{p})
. Our algorithmic achievability results hold for general signals with high probability in the sublinear sparsity regime
p\to\infty
,
n\to\infty
,
k=o(p)
, and
n=\omega(k)
.

Computational Lower Bounds for
\mathtt{MSLR}

We provide novel rigorous evidence through the study of low-degree polynomials
(
Kunisky et al. 2022
;
Schramm and Wein 2022
;
Hopkins 2018
)
that there exists a fundamental algorithmic barrier to solving a detection (hypothesis testing) variant of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
at all sample complexities
n=o(\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\log{p}})
and sparsities
k=o(\sqrt{p})
. Moreover, we show that this computational barrier implies a smooth tradeoff between sample and time complexities, preventing algorithms with running time less than
\exp(\tilde{\Theta}(\frac{k^{2}}{n}\cdot(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2}))
from succeeding. These results extend those of
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Fan et al. 2018
by showing that
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
has a significant statistical-to-computational gap in all
SNR
regimes (including the noiseless and
\texttt{SNR}=\omega(1)
regimes), and by identifying a smooth tradeoff between sample size and running time in the hard regime.

We then provide polynomial-time reductions between the detection and recovery variants of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, for signals taking nonzero values in
\{1,-1\}
, translating our hardness results to evidence that exact support recovery is just as hard for growing
SNR
values. We also show that any
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime containing
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
as a subproblem must be hard, by reducing the
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
exact recovery problem to exact recovery in the more general
Partially Symmetric Balanced

\mathtt{MSLR}
regime, or
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, where

\displaystyle\text{$\mathtt{PSB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}:\phi=\frac{1}{2},\text{ and }{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}=-{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}\text{ for }j\in J\subseteq{\rm supp}({\bm{\beta}}_{1})\cap{\rm supp}({\bm{\beta}}_{2}),\text{ with }|J|=\Theta(k).

(2)

Our computational lower bounds for the noiseless version of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
yield equivalent lower bounds for exact support recovery in sparse phase retrieval, where
{\bm{y}}=|{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}|+{\bm{w}}
.
This provides novel rigorous evidence of a computational barrier and a smooth information-computation tradeoff for solving exact support recovery in sparse phase retrieval with
n=\tilde{o}(k^{2})
samples, addressing a prominent open question on the hardness of this problem
(
Liu et al. 2021
;
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Wu and Rebeschini 2021
)
.

Algorithms for
\mathtt{MSLR}

Perhaps surprisingly, however, we prove that the above algorithmic barrier vanishes outside of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
. We show that a simple thresholding algorithm called
\mathtt{CORR}
solves the detection variant of
\mathtt{MSLR}
outside of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
with
O(np)
running time and sample complexity
n
of order
\frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\,\log{p}
, matching that required for efficiently solving sparse linear regression. We note that
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
is a very narrow parameter regime. Indeed, for signal priors (on the non-zero values) that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, the constraint (
1
) almost surely does not hold, and therefore,
\mathtt{CORR}
succeeds on a set of measure one.

In terms of the original recovery problem,
\mathtt{CORR}
is proven to exactly recover the joint support of both signals outside of a regime slightly broader than
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
(see Theorem
2.6
for a precise statement). Recovery of the joint support then reduces the problem to the dense or proportionally-sparse case (
k/p=n/p=\Theta(1)
) where existing algorithms can infer
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
exactly.
This extends the recent work of
Mazumdar and Pal 2022
which provides an exact joint support recovery algorithm for the case of binary signals (drawn from
\{0,1\}^{p}
) with sample complexity of order
\frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log^{3}{p}
. We highlight that the assumption of binary signals with all the non-zero entries equal to 1 is restrictive as it does not encompass the important regimes
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
,
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
where the problem is hard.
We can summarize the algorithmically hard parameter regimes in set notation as:

{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\footnotesize{``Low-degree hard detection''}}}}{{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}}}\subset\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\begin{subarray}{c}\text{\footnotesize{``Exact support recovery}}\\
\text{\footnotesize{is hard by reduction''}}\end{subarray}}}{{\text{$\mathtt{PSB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}}\subset\;\;\;\mathtt{MSLR}.

Lower Bounds and Algorithms for
\mathtt{SLR}

Our results also provide clarity into the computational barriers that arise in the special case of sparse linear regression (
\mathtt{SLR}
), where
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}={\bm{\beta}}_{2}
.
As mentioned in the introduction, previous authors have established that a sample complexity of at least order
k\log{p}
is required for efficient algorithms
(
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
)
, with matching algorithmic upper bound results available for the case
\texttt{SNR}\to\infty

(
Wainwright 2009b
;
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
)
.
We extend these findings and provide rigorous low-degree evidence that polynomial-time algorithms require sample complexity of order at least
n^{\mathtt{SLR}}_{\texttt{alg}}:=\frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{p}
for the detection variant of
\mathtt{SLR}
(in the regime where
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}
is of order
k
). Our proof technique consists of a vanilla low-degree calculation for
\mathtt{SLR}
; this is different from the approach of
Bandeira et al. 2022
, who established a connection between the low-degree method and the Franz-Parisi criterion to obtain computational lower bounds for
\mathtt{SLR}
. Our direct proof technique allows us to explicitly quantify the role of
SNR
in the problem.

Furthermore, we prove that
\mathtt{CORR}
solves both detection and signed support recovery in
\mathtt{SLR}
with
9n^{\mathtt{SLR}}_{\texttt{alg}}
samples, for all
SNR
scalings and general sparse signal priors. Moreover, it runs in
O(np)
time which can be significantly more efficient than alternative solutions such as the Lasso depending on the convergence criterion used
(
Wainwright 2009b
)
. This in turn certifies the order optimality of
\mathtt{CORR}
for exact signed support recovery in
\mathtt{SLR}
with respect to the class of algorithms that are analytic polynomials of the input of degree at most
O(\log{p})
(including spectral methods running in
O(\log{p})
iterations). We note that the statistical-computational gap in
\mathtt{SLR}
between
\frac{k\log(p/k)}{\log(1+\texttt{SNR})}
and
n^{\mathtt{SLR}}_{\texttt{alg}}
is only up to multiplicative constants unless
\texttt{SNR}=\omega(1)
.

Our contributions are summarized along with existing results in Table
1
below, for signals taking values in
\{-1,0,1\}
. In Table
1
,
n_{\texttt{IT}}
denotes the information-theoretic threshold for detection (and by reduction, recovery) and
n_{\texttt{alg}}
denotes the sample threshold for efficient algorithms. Importantly, we show that
\mathtt{MSLR}
behaves like
\mathtt{SLR}
outside of the narrow
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime, and reconcile existing results in the literature proving achievable sample complexity of order
k
in the binary case
(
Mazumdar and Pal 2022
)
but of order
k^{2}
in the general case
(
Städler et al. 2010
)
. These results lead us to believe that the
\frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
-to-
\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
gap arises from brittle symmetries in the signals, and that
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
and
\mathtt{SLR}
are computationally very different problems, the former only inefficiently solvable in high-dimensional settings.

.

1.2
Connections to Previous Work

Among the first works rigorously evidencing statistical-to-computational gaps was that of
Barak et al. 2016
who proved a tight computational lower bound for the Planted Clique (
\mathtt{PC}
) problem using the sum-of-squares (SOS) hierarchy. Based on the SOS method,
Hopkins 2018
then formulated a conjecture (a version of Conjecture
1.2
described in the next subsection) on the optimality of low-degree polynomials for hypothesis testing. This approach has yielded evidence for computational barriers in high-dimensional inference problems such as sparse PCA
(
Hopkins and Steurer 2017
;
Bandeira et al. 2020
)
. Other approaches to evidencing computational barriers include the failure of classes of algorithms such as statistical query
(
Diakonikolas et al. 2019
)
, local
(
Linial 1992
;
Gamarnik and Sudan 2017
)
and message passing algorithms
(
Zdeborova and Krzakala 2016
;
Krzakala et al. 2007
)
, and the reduction from variants of canonical “hard” problems such as Planted Clique
(
Berthet and Rigollet 2013
;
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
)
.

Notably, the problem of high-dimensional
\mathtt{MSLR}
has attracted attention as the special case of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
has been shown to exhibit a
k
-to-
k^{2}
statistical-to-computational gap, which we more precisely define as a
\frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
-to-
\frac{k^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
gap. This was identified through the study of average-case reductions from Planted Clique
(
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
)
and the statistical query model
(
Fan et al. 2018
)
. After noticing that no polynomial-time algorithms for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
were known to succeed below sample complexity
\tilde{\Theta}(k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2})
,
Fan et al. 2018
derived lower bounds on the information-theoretic and computational limits of an associated detection problem. Specifically, they proved that the information-theoretic minimal sample complexity is
n=\tilde{\Theta}(k/\texttt{SNR}^{2})
, while statistical query algorithms (and conjecturally polynomial-time algorithms) are proven to fail for all sample complexities below the larger threshold of
n=\tilde{o}\left(k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2}\right)
. This matches in order the failure threshold of many existing algorithms in the literature, although it has not been rigorously shown that the computational lower bound is tight.

Similarly,
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
proved that the associated detection problem we consider in this work (
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
) reduces to a variant of the
\mathtt{PC}
detection problem termed “Secret Leakage
\mathtt{PC}
” in a regime contained within sample complexity
n=o\left(k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2}\right)
. The detection version of Planted Clique can be formulated as that of identifying whether a clique of size
k
has been artifically “planted” in an Erdös-Rényi graph of size
n
. The problem can be solved by exhaustive search for
k=\Omega(\log n)
. The Planted Clique conjecture is that there is no polynomial time algorithm solving
\mathtt{PC}
if
k=o(\sqrt{n})
. There are a variety of sources of evidence for the
\mathtt{PC}
conjecture, see
Feldman et al. 2013
;
Barak et al. 2016
;
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
and the references therein.

The results above provide evidence for a
\frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
-to-
\frac{k^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
statistical-to-computational gap between the information-theoretic and the computational limits of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
.
More broadly, the work in
Brennan and Bresler 2020a
makes a step towards understanding the pervasiveness of
k
-to-
k^{2}
gaps in high-dimensional statistics by showing that efficient algorithms for learning mixtures with
k
-sparse means require at least
\tilde{\Omega}(k^{2})
sample complexity. In Theorem
2.2
, we sharpen the existing computational lower bounds for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, evidencing a
more extensive
\frac{k}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
-to-
\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
gap, which unlike earlier lower bounds, indicates a significant computational barrier even in the noiseless regime (
\texttt{SNR}=\infty
).

1.3
The Low-Degree Method

The low-degree method is a framework for obtaining lower bounds on the complexity of hypothesis testing problems, that emerged from the study of the sum-of-squares hierarchy
(
Barak et al. 2016
;
Hopkins et al. 2017
;
Hopkins and Steurer 2017
;
Hopkins 2018
)
. The low-degree method boils
down to rigorously ruling out the possibility of low-degree polynomial functions of the input for
solving a given hypothesis testing problem.
Consider the setting of simple binary hypothesis testing, where one seeks to to distinguish between two distributions
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, where
N
is the (potentially growing) problem size. Given a sample
{\bm{x}}
drawn from either
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
or
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
, the goal is to identify whether
{\bm{x}}
originated from the former or the latter through a hypothesis test. In our setting of
\mathtt{MSLR}
, we can view
N=np+n
as the total dimension of our data
({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
, and notice that
\log{N}=O(\log{p})
. We consider two notions of success in testing:

•

Strong Detection/Distinguishing
: the test succeeds with probability
1-o(1)
as
p\to\infty
.

•

Weak Detection/Distinguishing
: the test succeeds with probability
\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
for some constant
\epsilon>0
.

A
degree-D polynomial algorithm
denotes a sequence of (possibly random) multivariate polynomials
g_{N}:{\mathbb{R}}^{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of degree
D
, and
f_{\leq D}
we denotes the orthogonal projection of a function
f
onto the space of degree-
D
polynomials. Over the last decade, it has been established that for a large array of high-dimensional testing problems (including sparse PCA, planted clique, community detection, and many others), the class of degree-
O(\log{p})
polynomial algorithms is strictly as powerful as the best known polynomial-time algorithms
(
Bandeira et al. 2020
;
Ding et al. 2023
;
Hopkins 2018
;
Hopkins and Steurer 2017
;
Hopkins et al. 2017
;
Kunisky et al. 2022
)
. This is formalized in the following conjecture.

Conjecture 1.2

(The Low Degree Conjecture
Coja-Oghlan et al. 2022
;
Hopkins 2018
)
.

Define the chi-square divergence between
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
as
\chi^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}):={\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}_{N}({\bm{x}})}{d{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}({\bm{x}})}^{2}-1
, and let

\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
be its projection onto the space of degree-
D
polynomials.

•

If
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})=O(1)
for some
D=\omega(\log{N})
, strong detection has no polynomial-time algorithm and furthermore requires runtime
\exp(\tilde{\Omega}(D))
.

•

If
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})=o(1)
for some
D=\omega(\log{N})
, weak detection has no polynomial-time algorithm and furthermore requires runtime
\exp(\tilde{\Omega}(D))
.

A variety of state-of-the-art algorithms can be approximated by low-degree polynomials and therefore rigorously ruled out by low-degree lower bounds of the above form, including the important class of spectral methods (see Theorem 4.4 of
Kunisky et al. 2022
), and all statistical query algorithms
(
Brennan et al. 2021
)
. Recent works have also proven the equivalence between low-degree polynomial algorithms and well-established algorithmic solutions derived from statistical physics in certain classes of problems
(
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Montanari and Wein 2022
)
. Although degree
O(\log{p})
polynomials are not proven to encompass all polynomial-time algorithms, the success of such a polynomial in hypothesis testing tends to indicate the success of general polynomial-time algorithms. In this light, we aim to provide concrete evidence for computational hardness in
\mathtt{MSLR}
and
\mathtt{SLR}
by proving a low-degree lower bound of the form
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})=O(1)
for an associated detection problem, which can then be reduced to recovery. For more background on the low-degree method, see Appendix
A
.

2
Main Results

2.1
Lower bounds for
\mathtt{MSLR}

We begin by defining a detection variant of
\mathtt{MSLR}
, where given
({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
the goal is to distinguish between two hypotheses: one in which the data correspond to the
\mathtt{MSLR}
model, and another in which
{\bm{X}}
and
{\bm{y}}
are independent.

Definition 2.1

(Detection Variant
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
)
.

For
{\bm{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
,
\sigma>0
, and
{\bm{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the following hypothesis testing problem:

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}:\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{X}}\\
{\bm{y}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{X}}\\
\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}\cdot{\bm{w}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}:\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{X}}\\
{\bm{y}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}{\bm{X}}\\
\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}}\end{bmatrix}

where
({\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2})\sim\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
, and
X_{i,j}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
w_{i}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
z_{i}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\text{Bernoulli}(\phi)
. The task is to construct a function
f
which strongly distinguishes
{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})
from
{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
.

Notice that the marginal distributions of
{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})
and
{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
are equal, so as to rule out solutions that simply threshold the moments of
{\bm{y}}
and ignore
{\bm{X}}
. The corresponding detection variant of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, denoted by
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
, is defined similarly to
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
in the parameter regime of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
given in (
1
). From this formulation we obtain the following hardness result for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
. The proof is given in Appendix
B.3
.

Theorem 2.2

(Low-degree lower bound for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
)
.

Consider the setting of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
with
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
, and bounded amplitude signals (
{\bm{\beta}}_{\texttt{min}}=\Theta(\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\infty})
). For sample sizes
n
where
n=\omega(\max\{k,\log{p}\})
and
n=o\left(\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\log{p}}\right)
, Conjecture
1.2
implies that any randomized algorithm requires running time
\exp\left({\tilde{\Omega}\left(\min\left\{\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{n\texttt{SNR}^{2}},n\right\}\right)}\right)
to solve
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
in the regime
k=o(\sqrt{p})
.

Theorem
2.2
is our main low-degree hardness result. There are three regimes of interest, which we describe in terms of
n^{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}_{\texttt{alg}}:=\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}
. First, if
n=\Omega\left(n^{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}_{\texttt{alg}}/{\log{p}}\right)
, the lower bound on the running time in Theorem
2.2
equals
e^{\tilde{O}(\log{p})}
, and hence does not rule out polynomial-time solutions. Otherwise, Theorem
2.2
(via Conjecture
1.2
) implies a smooth tradeoff between sample size
n
and super-polynomial (but sub-exponential) running time
\exp\left(\tilde{\Omega}\left(n^{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}_{\texttt{alg}}/n\right)\right)
, for
n=\omega(({n^{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}_{\texttt{alg}}})^{\frac{1}{2}})
; this is reminiscent of a similar tradeoff in Sparse PCA
(
Ding et al. 2023
)
. In the third case, where
n=o(({n^{\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}}_{\texttt{alg}}})^{\frac{1}{2}})
, Theorem
2.2
implies that
e^{\tilde{\Omega}(n)}
running time is required. Thus there are three distinct computational regimes depending on the sample complexity
n
: the first permitting polynomial-time solutions, the second enforcing a smooth inversely related information-computation tradeoff, and the last implying an exponential increase in running time as the sample size increases. This extends the results of
(
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Fan et al. 2018
)
which indicated that the
n=\tilde{o}(k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2})
sample regime presents statistical-query and planted-clique related algorithmic barriers for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
with signals in
\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
; note that a lower bound of order
k^{2}/\texttt{SNR}^{2}
is vacuous in the noiseless setting, as well as in the natural setting where
\texttt{SNR}=\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=\Theta(k)
.

The work in
Fan et al. 2018
proved that the information-theoretic minimal sample complexity of
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
is
n=\tilde{\Theta}(k/\texttt{SNR}^{2})
, which is vacuous for
\texttt{SNR}=\omega(\sqrt{k})
. The information-theoretic minimal sample complexity of the related sparse phase retrieval (
\mathtt{SPR}
) detection problem, however, is known to be of order
k\log{p}
for a broad class of signal-to-noise ratios (see, for example, Theorem 3.2 in
Cai et al. 2016
and Section 6.1 in
Lecué and Mendelson 2015
). By straightforward reductions from
\mathtt{SLR}
to
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
to
\mathtt{SPR}
, one can show that the information-theoretic sample complexity of detection in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
lies between
\frac{k\log{(p/k)}}{\log{(1+\texttt{SNR})}}
and
k\log{p}
. In this light, Theorem
2.2
certifies a statistical-computational gap in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
of order at least
k
for broad
SNR
regimes.

We highlight that Theorem
2.2
rigorously rules out the success of analytic polynomials of the input of degree at most
O(\log{p})
, including spectral methods. The
k=o(\sqrt{p})
assumption is often standard for detection lower bounds where the signal is
k
-sparse (see
(
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
;
Fan et al. 2018
;
Ding et al. 2023
)
and references therein), and can at times be lifted by conditioning away a certain bad event
(
Bandeira et al. 2022
)
.

Remark 2.3
.

We have included the bounded amplitude assumption in Theorem
2.2
for interpretability. The dependence on
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\infty}
can be made explicit by replacing
k^{2}
in Theorem
2.2
with

\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{4}/\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\infty}^{4}
. We believe the dependence on
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\infty}
is an artifact of the proof technique; see Appendix
B.3
.

Through Theorem
C.3
in Appendix
C
, we provide a polynomial-time reduction from
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
to exact support recovery in
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, for signals in
\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
and
\texttt{SNR}=\omega(1)
, transferring hardness from Theorem
2.2
to this case. In Appendix
C.3
, we provide a polynomial-time reduction from
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
to both exact support recovery and detection in sparse phase retrieval (
\mathtt{SPR}
) for signals with non-zero entries in
\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
, translating the hardness results of Theorem
2.2
to
\mathtt{SPR}
. This provides novel rigorous evidence for the conjecture that
\mathtt{SPR}
is computationally infeasible for sample sizes
n=\tilde{o}(k^{2})

(
Wu and Rebeschini 2021
;
Li et al. 2022
;
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
)
.

2.2
Algorithms for
\mathtt{MSLR}

We denote the support sets of
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
by
\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}
, respectively. Note that
|\mathcal{S}_{1}|=|\mathcal{S}_{2}|=k
.
Let us define the following quantities:

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}:=\min_{j\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}}(\phi{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}+(1-\phi){\bm{\beta}}_{2,j})^{2},

\displaystyle\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{>0}:=\min\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}\\
(\phi{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}+(1-\phi){\bm{\beta}}_{2,j})>0\end{subarray}}(\phi{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}+(1-\phi){\bm{\beta}}_{2,j})^{2}.

Note that
\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{>0}>0
for
({\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2})\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
outside of the
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime. Also recall that
{\bm{\beta}}_{\texttt{min}}=\min\{|\beta|\;|\;\beta\in\mathcal{D}\}>0
.

Definition 2.4

(
\mathtt{CORR}
)
.

Let
\mathtt{CORR}
be the algorithm that outputs an estimate of the joint support set
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}
of
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
,
{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
according to
\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}}=\left\{j\in[p]:\,\left|\frac{\langle{\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{y}}\rangle}{\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}}\right|\geq\tau\right\}
, where
\tau=\sqrt{2(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2})\log{2p}}
for some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
.

Theorem 2.5

(Success of
\mathtt{CORR}
on
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
outside
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
)
.

Consider the general setting of
\mathtt{MSLR-D}\setminus\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}
with
({\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2})\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be the parameter used in
\mathtt{CORR}
. Then provided

n\geq\frac{32(1+\epsilon)}{\min\{\phi^{2}{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\texttt{min}},\,(1-\phi)^{2}{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\texttt{min}},\,\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{>0}\}}\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}\,(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{2p},

the
\mathtt{CORR}
algorithm solves strong detection in
\mathtt{MSLR-D}\setminus\text{$\mathtt{SB}$-$\mathtt{MSLR}$}
.

The proof of Theorem
2.5
is given in Appendix
D.2
. In the natural setting where
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}
is of order
k
, the theorem implies that

\mathtt{CORR}
solves
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
outside of the
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime with
square-root
the number of samples implied by the low-degree lower bound in Theorem
2.2
, up to
\log
factors. Indeed, the sample complexity in Theorem
2.5
matches the optimal sample complexity for the simpler
\mathtt{SLR-D}
problem; see Theorem
2.8
below. This theorem effectively quantifies the extent to which one can solve
\mathtt{MSLR}
with the sample complexity of
\mathtt{SLR}
. The proof of Theorem
2.5
also holds in the more general case where
\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{>0}>0
and the signal norms
\|{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\|_{2},\|{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\|_{2}
are not constrained to be equal. For signal priors on the nonzero entries that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, the event
\{\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{>0}>0\}
has measure one, as
\phi{\bm{\beta}}_{1}+(1-\phi){\bm{\beta}}_{2}\neq 0
is almost surely satisfied.

Theorem 2.6

(Sucess of
\mathtt{CORR}
for recovery in
\mathtt{MSLR}
for
\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}>0
)
.

Consider the general setting of
\mathtt{MSLR}
with either
\sigma=0,\phi\neq 1/2
(noiseless), or
\phi=1/2,\texttt{SNR}=\Omega(k)
(balanced). Let
({\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2})\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be the parameter used in
\mathtt{CORR}
, and

n\geq\frac{32(1+\epsilon)}{\min\{\phi^{2}{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\texttt{min}},(1-\phi)^{2}{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\texttt{min}},\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}\}}\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{2p}.

Then there exists an algorithm which, in combination with
\mathtt{CORR}
, exactly recovers
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
and
{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
(up to relabeling) with probability at least
1-c_{1}(\frac{k}{p}+ke^{-c_{2}n}+\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{p^{c_{2}}})
for constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
.

The proof of Theorem
2.6
, given in Appendix
D.3
, first uses
\mathtt{CORR}
for support recovery, followed by existing recovery algorithms for the noiseless and balanced cases of dense (
k/p=\Theta(1)
) mixed linear regression
(
Yi et al. 2014
;
Chen et al. 2014
)
. Under the condition
\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}>0
, which is slightly more restrictive than
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, Theorem
2.6
yields a sample complexity of the same order as that for
\mathtt{SLR}
.
We note that for signal priors on the nonzero entries that are absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, the event
\{\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}>0\}
has measure one. We highlight that the noiseless case can be formulated as a mixed variant of compressed sensing with independent Gaussian design
(
Yu and Sapiro 2011
)
.

Remark 2.7
.

The restriction to the noiseless and balanced cases in Theorem
2.6
is due to the guarantees provided by existing algorithms in the dense case, for which experiments indicate success far beyond these regimes
(
Yi et al. 2014
;
Chen et al. 2014
)
.

2.3
Lower bounds for Sparse Linear Regression (
\mathtt{SLR}
)

We define the detection variant of
\mathtt{SLR}
, called
\mathtt{SLR-D}
, as per Definition
2.1
with the constraint
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}={\bm{\beta}}_{2}
. The following lower bound for
\mathtt{SLR-D}
is proved in Appendix
B.2
.

Theorem 2.8

(Low-degree lower bound for
\mathtt{SLR-D}
)
.

Consider the setting of
\mathtt{SLR-D}
(Definition
2.1
under
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}={\bm{\beta}}_{2}
) with
{\bm{\beta}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}\left(\mathcal{D}\right)
.
For
n=\omega(\log{p})
and
n\leq(1-\epsilon)(1-2\theta)\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{2}}{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{\infty}}\frac{(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{p}
for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
,
Conjecture
1.2
implies that any randomized algorithm requires running time
e^{\tilde{\Omega}\left(n\right)}

to solve
\mathtt{SLR-D}
in the regime
k=O(p^{\theta})\leq\sqrt{p}
with
\theta\in(0,1/2]
.

In the natural setting where
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}
is of order
k
and the entries have bounded amplitude, the low-degree lower bound on
n
is of order
\frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log p
. This matches the order of existing lower bounds for
\mathtt{SLR}
in
(
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
)
, but has the advantage of being valid for all
SNR
regimes and generic priors on the sparse signal
{\bm{\beta}}
. We believe that the dependence of the bound on
\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{\infty}
is an artifact of the proof technique; see Appendix
B.2
.

A reduction from
\mathtt{SLR-D}
to
\mathtt{SLR}
follows similarly to the reduction from
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
to
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
, which is given in Appendix
C
.

2.4
Algorithms for
\mathtt{SLR}

Theorem 2.9
.

Consider the setting of
\mathtt{SLR}
with
{\bm{\beta}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be the parameter used in
\mathtt{CORR}
. Then for
n\geq\frac{8(1+\epsilon)}{{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\min}}\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}\frac{(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{2p}
, we have that
\mathtt{CORR}
solves strong detection in
\mathtt{SLR-D}
.

We next consider a slight variant of
\mathtt{CORR}

that recovers the
signed
support of
{\bm{\beta}}
. It produces
\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}
with entries given by

\displaystyle\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}_{j}=\mathbbm{1}\left\{\left|\frac{\langle{\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{y}}\rangle}{\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}}\right|\geq\sqrt{2(1+\epsilon/2)\log{2p}}\right\}\text{sign}\left(\frac{\langle{\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{y}}\rangle}{\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}}\right),\ \text{ for }j\in[p],

(3)

where
\text{sign}(x)
equals
1
for
x>0
, equals
-1
for
x<0
, and
0
for
x=0
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Consider the setting of
\mathtt{SLR}
with
{\bm{\beta}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
. Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
be the parameter used in the above variant of
\mathtt{CORR}
. Then for
n\geq\frac{8(1+\epsilon)}{{\bm{\beta}}^{2}_{\min}}\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}\frac{(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\texttt{SNR}}\log{2p}
, the vector
\hat{{\bm{\beta}}}
in (
3
) equals the signed support of
{\bm{\beta}}
with probability at least
1-(\frac{k}{p}+2ke^{-c_{2}n}+\frac{1}{p^{c_{2}}})
for some constant
c_{2}>0
.

The proofs of Theorem
2.9
and Theorem
2.10
are given in Appendix
D.4
. The sample complexity required for the success of
\mathtt{CORR}
matches the low-degree lower bound in Theorem
2.8
up to constants, which rigorously certifies the order optimality of
\mathtt{CORR}
among low-degree polynomial algorithms, including spectral methods running in
O(\log{p})
iterations, in all
SNR
regimes. These achievable sample complexities also match those of previous work
(
Wainwright 2009b
;
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Gamarnik and Zadik 2022
;
Cai and Wang 2011
;
Donoho and Tanner 2010
)
, with the important extension that they hold for all
SNR
scalings and general sparse signal priors.

3
Proof Ideas

Low-Degree Lower Bounds

Theorem
2.2
amounts to proving that
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\|{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}}))=O(1)
in
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
(Definition
2.1
) with
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}=-{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
and
\phi=1/2
, for
n
in the regime specified in the theorem. We rewrite the expression for
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\|{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}}))
in Conjecture
1.2
in terms of multivariate Hermite polynomials in the data
({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})
of degree up to
D
. For
\bm{\alpha}=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{np+n}]
, with
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, the normalized Hermite polynomial of order
\bm{\alpha}
is denoted by

\frac{\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}}!}
. The precise definition of the polynomial is given in Appendix
B
, but the key fact we will use is that
\Big\{\frac{\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}}!}\Big\}
form an orthonormal system with respect to the null distribution in Definition
2.1
(see Proposition
B.5
in Appendix
B
). Using this, we have

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\|{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}}))+1

\displaystyle={\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}{d({\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}}))}\right)_{\leq D}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq|\bm{\alpha}|\leq D}\frac{1}{\bm{\alpha}!}\,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\left[\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}{d({\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}}))}\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq|\bm{\alpha}|\leq D}\frac{1}{\bm{\alpha}!}\,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}\left[\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\right]^{2},

(4)

where
\bm{\alpha}!=\prod_{i}\alpha_{i}!
.
The key element of the proof involves subsequently upper bounding (
4
) through Hermite polynomial identities and multinomial-theorem manipulations, yielding a weighted sum over
D
moments of the overlap
\langle{\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{(1)},{\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{(2)}\rangle
, where
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{(1)},{\bm{\beta}}_{1}^{(2)}
are two i.i.d copies of the signal
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}
(see Lemma
B.11
). Each of these
D
moments can be bounded for
k\leq\sqrt{p}
, allowing the entire sum over
D\simeq\min\left\{\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{n\texttt{SNR}^{2}},n\right\}
terms to converge, and yielding the result. The case of
\mathtt{SLR}
in Theorem
2.8
is similar, but with the simplification
{\bm{\beta}}_{1}={\bm{\beta}}_{2}
, we can afford to set
D\simeq n
and still have this sum converge, yielding the key difference in lower bounds between
\mathtt{SLR}
and
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
.

Reductions from detection to recovery

We follow the procedure for average-case reductions outlined by
Brennan and Bresler 2020b
. We transfer computational hardness from
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
to recovery in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
by forming an average-case reduction for
k
-sparse signals in
\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
. Denote the parameter regime of Theorem
2.2
as the “critical” parameter regime. Given any sequence of parameters
\mathcal{P}
in the critical regime, we construct another sequence of parameters
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
in the critical regime with the following property: if there exists a randomized polynomial-time algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
solving exact recovery in
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
with parameter scaling
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, then we can construct a randomized polynomial-time algorithm solving
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
with parameter scaling
\mathcal{P}
. This would in turn contradict Theorem
2.2
, implying computational hardness of exact recovery in
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
in the critical regime. We first provide an average case reduction from
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR-D}
to exact recovery in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
in Lemma
C.1
, and then reduce exact recovery in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
to exact recovery in
\mathtt{PSB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
in Theorem
C.3
.

The
\mathtt{CORR}
algorithm

For
\mathtt{MSLR}
, the proofs of Theorems
2.5
and
2.6
crucially rely on Theorem
D.1
, which shows that
\mathtt{CORR}
recovers the joint support of the signals (
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}
) if
n
satisfies the condition in Theorem
2.6
. To prove Theorem
D.1
, we analyze the quantity
u_{j}:=\frac{\langle{\bm{X}}_{j},{\bm{y}}\rangle}{\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}}
in three cases. When
j\in(\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2})^{\complement}
, we have that
u_{j}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
for
j\in[p]
by the independence of
{\bm{X}}_{j}
and
{\bm{y}}
. The typical value of
\max_{j\in[p]}u_{j}
in this case is
\sqrt{2\log{p}}
, and we can bound the probability of a false positive by standard concentration bounds, detailed in Lemma
D.9
.
When
j\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}
, we show that conditioned on
{\bm{y}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
,
u_{j}
is normally distributed with mean

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\left[u_{j}\mid{\bm{y}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\right]=\frac{\|{\bm{y}}_{\{{\bm{z}}=1\}}\|_{2}^{2}{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}+\|{\bm{y}}_{\{{\bm{z}}=0\}}\|^{2}_{2}{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}}{\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}(\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}+\sigma^{2})},

(5)

and variance less than
1
(Lemma
D.4
). Here,
\|{\bm{y}}_{\{{\bm{z}}=1\}}\|_{2}
denotes the norm of the vector with entries
(y_{i}1_{\{z_{i}=1\}})_{i\in[n]}
. For large
n,p
and
j\in[p]
, the typical value of the conditional mean above is
\sqrt{\frac{n}{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}+\sigma^{2}}}\,(\phi{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}+(1-\phi){\bm{\beta}}_{2,j})
, which is greater than
\sqrt{2(1+\epsilon/2)\log{2p}}
for

n\geq\frac{2(1+\epsilon)}{\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}}(\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}+\sigma^{2})\log{2p}\simeq(1+\epsilon)\frac{k(\texttt{SNR}+1)}{\langle{\bm{\beta}}\rangle^{2}_{\texttt{min}}\texttt{SNR}}\log{2p}.

The remaining case
j\in\mathcal{S}_{1}\Delta\mathcal{S}_{2}
is similar, with the conditional mean obtained by setting
{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}=0
in (
5
). The results for
\mathtt{SLR}
in Theorems
2.9
,
2.10
follow a similar reasoning, with
{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\left[u_{j}\mid{\bm{y}},{\bm{z}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\right]=\frac{{\bm{\beta}}_{j}\|{\bm{y}}\|_{2}}{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}+\sigma^{2}}
.

4
Discussion

In this work we rigorously characterize the computational hardness of Mixed Sparse Linear Regression (
\mathtt{MSLR}
) through the method of low-degree polynomials. We evidence that in the highly symmetric
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime, randomized polynomial-time algorithms cannot solve an associated detection problem with sample complexity
n=\tilde{o}\left(\frac{k^{2}(\texttt{SNR}+1)^{2}}{\texttt{SNR}^{2}}\right)
, revealing a statistical-computational gap of order at least
k
. Outside of the
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
regime, however, a simple polynomial-time algorithm
\mathtt{CORR}
succeeds in solving detection with minimal sample complexity.

We note that our low-degree statistical-computational gap for
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
persists even in the noiseless (
\texttt{SNR}=\infty
) regime. Recent discoveries have highlighted that evidence for statistical-to-computational gaps do not always hold in the noiseless setting. Examples include “brittle” algorithms such as Gaussian elimination “breaking” the statistical-to-computational gap in learning parities
(
Zadik et al. 2022
)
. It was also recently found in
Zadik et al. 2022
that the LLL family of algorithms, originating from cryptography, can break the statistical-to-computational gaps predicted in certain noiseless clustering problems. Further, in a recent talk by
Zadik 2021
, a proof sketch was presented for a lattice-based algorithm that can recover
{\bm{\beta}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
in
dense
noiseless phase retrieval with
p+1
measurements — this breaks a conjectured statistical-to-computational gap for dense phase retrieval, but the algorithm does not capture the sparse problem structure present in
\mathtt{MSLR}
. To the best of our knowledge, noiseless inference in
\mathtt{SB}
-
\mathtt{MSLR}
and sparse phase retrieval still cannot be achieved with fewer than order
k^{2}\log{p}
samples for the case of
k
-sparse
{\bm{\beta}}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{p}
. Such an achievement, if possible, would constitute an interesting and novel contribution.

Appendix A
Additional background on the Low Degree Method

In this section, we give additional background on the low-degree method, the chi-squared divergence and its orthogonal projection onto the space of low-degree polynomials. Consider the setting in Section
1.3
, where the task is to distinguish between
two probability distributions
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
over
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
where
N
is the (potentially growing) problem size. Given a sample
{\bm{x}}
drawn from
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
or
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
, one seeks to identify whether
{\bm{x}}
originated from the former or the latter through a hypothesis test. Recall the notions of strong and weak detection from Section
1.3
.

One powerful method of identifying whether strong or weak detection is possible is through the study of the
chi-squared divergence

\chi^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
. Indeed, assume that
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
, and let
\mathsf{L}=\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}{d{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}
be the likelihood ratio. We have:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})

\displaystyle:={\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}\,\mathsf{L}({\bm{x}})^{2}-1

\displaystyle=\sup_{f:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}}\frac{({\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})^{2}}-1

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}\\
{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left({\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})\right)^{2}}{{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})^{2}},

where the equivalences follow from standard arguments (see
Kunisky et al. 2022
). Interpreting the above result, the chi-squared divergence represents optimality in the
L^{2}
sense. It relates to the squared maximum expectation any function can have under
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
, while still being bounded in the space
L^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
. In fact, the chi-square divergence between two distributions can rigorously characterize their behaviour under testing:

Lemma A.1

(Adapted from Lemma 2 of
Montanari et al. 2015
and Lemma 7.1 of
Coja-Oghlan et al. 2022
)
.

•

If
\chi^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})=O(1)
as
N\to\infty
, then strong detection is impossible.

•

If
\chi^{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})=o(1)
as
N\to\infty
, then weak detection is impossible.

This result is powerful, as it identifies the chi-square divergence as a sufficient quantity for finding identifying results in testing. Note however, that this quantity reveals nothing with regards to
computation
.

The computational analogue of the chi-square divergence is the
degree-
D
chi-square divergence

\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
. This quantity measures whether
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
and
{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}
can be distinguished by a degree-
D
polynomial of the input
{\bm{x}}
. Consider the Hilbert Space
L^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
, where for functions
f,g:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}
we have the inner product
\langle f,g\rangle:={\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}Q_{N}}[f({\bm{x}})g({\bm{x}})]
and the corresponding norm
\|f\|_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}}
. Additionally, denote
{\mathbb{R}}[{\bm{x}}]_{\leq D}
as the space of multivariate polynomials from
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
of degree at most
D
, and let
f^{\leq D}
denote the orthogonal projection of
f
onto
R[{\bm{x}}]_{\leq D}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
. We can then define
\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
as follows:

\displaystyle\chi^{2}_{\leq D}({\mathbb{P}}_{N}\|{\mathbb{Q}}_{N})

\displaystyle:={\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}\mathsf{L}^{\leq D}({\bm{x}})^{2}-1

(6)

\displaystyle=\|\mathsf{L}^{\leq D}\|^{2}_{Q_{N}}-1

\displaystyle=\sup_{f\in{\mathbb{R}}[{\bm{x}}]_{\leq D}}\frac{({\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}}))^{2}}{{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})^{2}}-1

\displaystyle=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\mathbb{R}}[{\bm{x}}]_{\leq D}\\
{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\left({\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})\right)^{2}}{{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\bm{x}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{Q}}_{N}}f({\bm{x}})^{2}}.

The proof of this result can be found in
Hopkins 2018
;
Kunisky et al. 2022
. The low-degree chi-square divergence can therefore interpreted analogously to chi-square divergence: it quantifies the maximum expectation any low-degree function can have under
{\mathbb{P}}_{N}
while still being in the degree-
D
polynomial subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{Q}}_{N})
. We then have the analogue of Lemma
A.1
for low-degree polynomial functions of the input and, conjecturally, general polynomial-time algorithms, given by Conjecture
1.2
.

In this work, we consider testing between distributions that do not simply consist of
signal plus noise
, but instead of linearly transformed signals plus noise. Along with the recent work in
Bandeira et al. 2022
;
Arpino 2021
, this is, to the best of our knowledge, among the first applications of the low-degree method to such problems, which were previously believed to be out of reach from current methods
(
Schramm and Wein 2022
)
.

Appendix B
Proofs of Low-Degree Lower Bounds

Preliminaries and Notation.

All results concerning the low-degree hardness of the associated problems are asymptotic in
p
, as we take
p\to\infty
first. We use the conventions from
Schramm and Wein 2022
. Let
\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,\cdots\}
and
\left[n\right]=\{1,2\cdots n\}
. We define
0^{0}:=1
. We denote by boldface a multiset or vector, so for
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
we mean
\bm{\alpha}=[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n}]
for
\alpha_{i}\in\mathbb{N},\forall i\in[n]
. For
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, define
|\bm{\alpha}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}
,
\bm{\alpha}!=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}!
and (for
\bm{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
)
\bm{X}^{\bm{\alpha}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}
. Let
\texttt{abs}(\bm{\alpha})
denote the entry-wise absolute value operation on the vector
\bm{\alpha}
. We use
\bm{\alpha}\geq\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}
to mean
\alpha_{i}\geq{\bm{\beta}}_{i}
for all
i
. The operations
\bm{\alpha}+\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}
and
\bm{\alpha}-\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}
are performed entrywise. For
\bm{\alpha},\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
with
\bm{\alpha}\geq\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}
, define
{\bm{\alpha}\choose\bm{{\bm{\beta}}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}{\alpha_{i}\choose{\bm{\beta}}_{i}}
. We use subindices to denote subsets of a vector or multiset, so for
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times(p+1)}
, we let
\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}:=[\alpha_{1,p+1},\ldots,\alpha_{n,p+1}]
denote the
p+1
th column of the matrix
\bm{\alpha}
. We let
\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}
denote the entire
n\times p
submatrix obtained by selecting only up to the
p
th column, and
\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}
the vector consisting of elements from the
i
th row up to the
p
th column. We denote by
[{\bm{A}}\;\;{\bm{y}}]
the matrix formed through the horizontal concatenation of
{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
onto
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
, forming an
n\times(p+1)
real matrix. Unless otherwise indicated, we let
\|\cdot\|:=\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle:=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
. We use
\mathbbm{1}
to denote the indicator function.

The univariate Hermite polynomials
H_{k}(x)
for
k\geq 0
are defined by the recursion
H_{0}(x)=1
, and

H_{k+1}(x)=xH_{k}(x)-H_{k}^{\prime}(x)
.
For
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{N}
, let
H_{\bm{\alpha}}
denote the
multivariate
Hermite polynomial of order
\bm{\alpha}
, defined as
H_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{u}})=\prod_{i=1}^{N}H_{\alpha_{i}}(u_{i})
, for
{\bm{u}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the normalized
N
-variate Hermite polynomials
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}}!}{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}
form a complete orthonormal system of (multivariate) polynomials for
L^{2}(\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\bm{I}_{N}))
(see
Kunisky et al. 2022
).

In what follows, we give further basic facts regarding Hermite polynomials (see
Kunisky et al. 2022
for more detailed descriptions), along with two auxiliary combinatorial lemmas that will be of use for the main proofs.

Proposition B.1

(Gaussian Integration by Parts, Prop. 2.10 in
Kunisky et al. 2022
)
.

If
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is
k
-times continuously differentiable and
f(y)
and its first
k
derivatives are bounded by
\mathcal{O}\left(\exp(|y|^{\alpha})\right)
for some
\alpha\in(0,2)
, then

\mathbb{E}_{y\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\left[H_{k}(y)f(y)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{y\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\left[\frac{d^{k}f}{dy^{k}}(y)\right].

Proposition B.2

(Hermite derivative
(
Jakimovski et al. 2006
)
)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

H_{n}^{(m)}(x)=\frac{n!}{(n-m)!}H_{n-m}(x).

Proposition B.3

(Hermite sum formula, Prop 3.1 in
Schramm and Wein 2022
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
z,\mu\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle H_{k}(z+\mu)=\sum_{l=0}^{k}{k\choose l}\mu^{k-l}H_{l}(z).

Proposition B.4

(Hermite multiplication formula
Oldham et al. 2009
)
.

For
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,

H_{n}(\gamma x)=\sum_{i=0}^{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor}\gamma^{n-2i}\left(\gamma^{2}-1\right)^{i}\binom{n}{2i}\frac{(2i)!}{i!}2^{-i}H_{n-2i}(x).

Proposition B.5
.

Consider the null distribution
{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})
whose law given by

\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\otimes n\times p}\otimes\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1\right)^{\otimes(p+1)}.

Let
{\bm{u}}=[{\bm{X}}\ {\bm{y}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times(p+1)}
. Then, an orthonormal system with respect to this null distribution, indexed by
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times(p+1)}
, is given by

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}}!}\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{u}}):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}H_{\alpha_{i,j}}(u_{i,j})H_{\alpha_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{u_{i,p+1}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\bm{\alpha}^{(1)},\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times(p+1)}
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}}!}\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}}({\bm{u}})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}}!}\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}}({\bm{u}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}!\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}!}}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,j}}(u_{i,j})H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{u_{i,p+1}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,j}}(u_{i,j})H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{u_{i,p+1}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}!\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}!}}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,j}}(u_{i,j})H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,j}}\left(u_{i,j}\right){\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{u_{i,p+1}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{u_{i,p+1}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}!\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}!}}{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,j}}(u_{i,j})H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,j}}\left(u_{i,j}\right){\mathop{\mathbb{E}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,p+1}}\left(w_{i}\right)H_{\alpha^{(2)}_{i,p+1}}(w_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}!\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}!}}\sqrt{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}!\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,j}=\alpha^{(2)}_{i,j}\}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha^{(1)}_{i,p+1}=\alpha^{(2)}_{i,p+1}\}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\bm{\alpha}^{(1)}=\bm{\alpha}^{(2)}},

where
w_{i}\mathbin{\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are independent of all other variables for
i\in[n]
.
∎

Lemma B.6
.

For
{\bm{\beta}}\in\mathbb{N}
even:

\sum_{\xi=0}^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}\binom{{\bm{\beta}}}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\xi}({\bm{\beta}}-2\xi-1)!!=\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\beta}}=0}.

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\xi=0}^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}\binom{{\bm{\beta}}}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^{\xi}({\bm{\beta}}-2\xi-1)!!

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi=0}^{{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}}\frac{{\bm{\beta}}!}{\xi!({\bm{\beta}}-2\xi)!!}\left(\frac{-1}{2}\right)^{\xi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\xi=0}^{{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}}\frac{{\bm{\beta}}!}{\xi!\cdot(\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}-\xi)!\cdot 2^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}-\xi}}\left(\frac{-1}{2}\right)^{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{\beta}}!}{(\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2})!\cdot 2^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}}\sum_{\xi=0}^{{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}}\binom{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}{\xi}\left(-1\right)^{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\bm{\beta}}!}{(\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2})!\cdot 2^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}}(1+(-1))^{\frac{{\bm{\beta}}}{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{{\bm{\beta}}=0}.

∎

Lemma B.7
.

For
p\geq 4
and
k\leq\sqrt{p}
, it holds that
\frac{p^{k}}{4k!}\leq\binom{p}{k}
.

Proof.

Note that
\binom{p}{k}\geq\frac{p^{k}}{4k!}
if and only if:

\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{j}{p}\right)\geq\frac{1}{4}

Then applying the
k\leq\sqrt{p}
assumption:

\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{j}{p}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\prod_{i=1}^{\lfloor\sqrt{p}\rfloor}\left(1-\frac{j}{p}\right)\geq\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{\sqrt{p}}

Now notice that for
\sqrt{p}\geq 2
, we have that
(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}})^{\sqrt{p}}\geq\frac{1}{4}
, leading to the desired result.
∎

B.1
Low-degree analysis for
\mathtt{MSLR}
: general mixtures

In subsection, we prove two technical lemmas. The first (Lemma
B.8
) derives an expression for the projection of the likelihood ratio onto the multivariate Hermite polynomial
\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}
defined in Proposition
B.5
. The second lemma (Lemma
B.9
) derives an explicit expression for the low-degree chi-squared divergence.

Lemma B.8
.

Let
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}}(\mathcal{D})
. Let
\mathsf{L}=\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}
be the likelihood ratio,
and
\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}
the Hermite polynomial defined in Proposition
B.5
. Then, for
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{n\times(p+1)}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}|}\|{\bm{\beta}}\|_{2}^{|\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}|-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}!

\displaystyle\quad\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|=0\}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}\right].

Proof.

We begin by expanding the inner product:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\left[\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}{d{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}})\otimes{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{y}})}\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},{\bm{y}})}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{p}H_{\alpha_{i,j}}(X_{i,j})\right)H_{\alpha_{i,p+1}}\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}}\right)_{i}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)\right],

and applying Gaussian Integration by Parts (Proposition
B.1
) we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|)!}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}H_{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha_{i,:p}}|}\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}}\right)_{i}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|)!}

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}H_{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha_{i,:p}}|}\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}}\right)_{i}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right).

We then apply the Hermite multiplication and addition formulas outlined in Propositions
B.3
and
B.4
:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\alpha_{i,:p}|)!}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\xi=0}^{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}-1\right)^{\xi}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}2^{-\xi}

\displaystyle\cdot H_{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})+{\bm{w}}\right)_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}},{\bm{w}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\alpha_{i,:p}|)!}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\xi=0}^{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}-1\right)^{\xi}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}2^{-\xi}

\displaystyle\cdot\hskip-20.00003pt\sum_{\eta=0}^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}{\eta}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})\right)_{i}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi-\eta}\hskip-20.00003pt\underbrace{H_{\eta}\left(w_{i}\right)}_{\text{$\neq 0$ only if $\eta=0$}},

which we simplify by noting that
H_{\eta}\left(w_{i}\right)\neq 0
only if
\eta=0
to obtain:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\alpha_{i,:p}|)!}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\xi=0}^{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\left(\frac{1}{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}-1\right)^{\xi}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}2^{-\xi}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})\right)_{i}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}.

Now switching the sum with the product and grouping terms we obtain:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}!}{(\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\alpha_{i,:p}|)!}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{[{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}}]\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},y)}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{\xi=0}^{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi}\frac{(2\xi)!}{\xi!}\left(\frac{\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+\frac{1}{\sigma}{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})\right)_{i}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi}\left(\frac{-\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}{2\big(\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1\big)}\right)^{\xi}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|}\frac{\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}!}{(\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|)!}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{[{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}}]\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},y)}\sum_{0\leq\bm{\xi}\leq\bm{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}

\displaystyle\cdot\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi_{i}}\frac{(2\xi_{i})!}{\xi_{i}!}\left({\frac{-\frac{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}}{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}{2}}\right)^{\xi_{i}}\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi_{i}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}\left({\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{1}\odot{\bm{z}}+{\bm{X}}{\bm{\beta}}_{2}\odot(1-{\bm{z}})\right)_{i}^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi_{i}}\right],

which by simplification and expansion then leads us to

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,p+1}|}\frac{\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}!}{(\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|)!}\sum_{0\leq\bm{\xi}\leq\bm{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi_{i}}\frac{(2\xi_{i})!}{\xi_{i}!}\left(\frac{-\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{2}\right)^{\xi_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{[{\bm{X}},{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2},{\bm{z}}]\mathbin{\overset{}{\kern 0.0pt\sim}}{\mathbb{P}}({\bm{X}},y)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}X_{i,j}{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{p}X_{i,j}{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi_{i}}\right].

Bringing out the expectation with respect to
{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}
we then obtain:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{L},\tilde{H}_{\bm{\alpha}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,p+1}|}\frac{\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}!}{(\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|)!}\sum_{0\leq\bm{\xi}\leq\bm{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\binom{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2\xi_{i}}\frac{(2\xi_{i})!}{\xi_{i}!}\left(\frac{-\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{2}\right)^{\xi_{i}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{X}},{\bm{z}}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{p}X_{i,j}{\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{p}X_{i,j}{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|-2\xi_{i}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\frac{1}{\sigma}}{\sqrt{\frac{\|{\bm{\beta}}\|^{2}_{2}}{\sigma^{2}}+1}}\right)^{|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,p+1}|}\frac{\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}!}{(\bm{\alpha}_{p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{\cdot,:p}|)!}\sum_{0\leq\bm{\xi}\leq\bm{\lfloor\frac{\alpha_{i,p+1}-|\bm{\alpha}_{i,:p}|}{2}\rfloor}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{{\bm{\beta}}_{1},{\bm{\beta}}_{2}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{p}\left({\bm{\beta}}_{1,j}z_{i}+{\bm{\beta}}_{2,j}(1-z_{i})\right)^{\alpha_{i,j}}\right)
